It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.

I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.\n\nIt’s more that the house has become harder to keep up with than it should be. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.

It isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.\n\nIt’s more that the house has become harder to keep up with than it should be. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.

It’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.\n\nIt’s more that the house has become harder to keep up with than it should be. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. And I’ve started paying people to do little maintenance jobs I probably should be able to manage myself, which makes owning the house more expensive than it needs to be.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “not doing as well,” but it does feel like I’m avoiding things I really ought to be dealing with.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it would probably help, yes… if I absolutely knew there couldn’t be any spiders, I imagine I’d relax quite a bit.

But I’m not sure it’s only the actual spider, if that makes sense. I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. It’s just that as soon as I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening, which is embarrassing to admit.

So yes, a completely spider-free place would likely make it better, but the fear seems to come up very quickly once I think there might be one around. Is that the kind of answer you mean?

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.\n\nIt’s more that the house has become harder to keep up with than it should be. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. And I’ve started paying people to do little maintenance jobs I probably should be able to manage myself, which makes owning the house more expensive than it needs to be.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “not doing as well,” but it does feel like I’m avoiding things I really ought to be dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would decrease significantly if you were in a completely different environment, like a spider-free zone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help, yes… if I absolutely knew there couldn’t be any spiders, I imagine I’d relax quite a bit.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s only the actual spider, if that makes sense. I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. It’s just that as soon as I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nSo yes, a completely spider-free place would likely make it better, but the fear seems to come up very quickly once I think there might be one around. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. It’s just that as soon as I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nSo yes, a completely spider-free place would likely make it better, but the fear seems to come up very quickly once I think there might be one around. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d like to say it would be much better somewhere else, and maybe it would be a bit. But I don’t think the house is the whole issue.

I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer, it just feels… enormous, and threatening, much more than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed admitting that.

So I suppose in a different house or location it might be less intense, but I’m not sure it would completely go away. Does that answer what you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it feels a bit silly to say out loud, but I suppose I’ve been arranging things at home around avoiding spiders. I mean, I try to manage it, but I put off doing laundry because it’s in the basement, and I avoid going into the garden shed. Sometimes I even pay someone to do small jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel when you see a spider or think about spiders being in a place you need to go?\n\nPatient: I know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do understand that. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I need to go down there, it suddenly feels… much bigger, I suppose, and threatening. More than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that, because it sounds over the top, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience panic or feel like you're having a heart attack when you see a spider or are in a situation where you might encounter one?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a heart attack, exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But yes, if I actually see one, my chest tightens straight away, and my hands can start shaking. Sometimes I just freeze in the doorway, or I’m backing out before I’ve even properly thought about it. It’s like my body decides for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe what happens when you see a spider as a panic attack?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably, if that’s the right word for it. I don’t want to overstate it, but when I actually spot one, my chest tightens immediately and my hands start shaking. I can freeze in the doorway, or I’ll back out so quickly it feels like my body’s decided before I’ve even had time to think.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear primarily of the spider itself or of the potential harm it could cause you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the spider itself, really… or maybe what my mind turns it into. I do know, logically, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I need to go there, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. I’m a bit embarrassed to admit that, because I know it probably sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders limited to specific situations or places, or is it more of a general fear that occurs regardless of where you are or what you're doing?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly in the places where I think they’re likely to be, if that makes sense. I’m not walking around every minute thinking about spiders, but at home I do find myself sort of organizing things around avoiding them.\n\nI put off going down to the basement to do laundry, and I avoid the garden shed if I can. And, I feel a bit silly saying this, but I’ve even paid people to do small jobs that I actually know how to do myself, just because I couldn’t quite make myself go into those spaces. It’s not very practical, I know.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships or work in any way?\n\nPatient: Not work, really… I mean, I manage fine at school, as far as I can tell. Relationships, I don’t think it’s caused arguments exactly.\n\nIt’s more that the house has become harder to keep up with than it should be. I’ll put off little chores, or avoid certain parts of the property, and then things pile up. And I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things I probably ought to be able to manage myself. It’s a bit embarrassing to say, but it does make owning the house more expensive and… more stressful than I’d like. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided traveling or going on vacation because you might encounter spiders in a new or unfamiliar place?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve avoided vacations specifically, no… not in a way I could point to. It’s more at home, really, where I know the places I’m likely to run into them. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in — I keep arranging the house around not seeing spiders. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. And I’ve started paying people to do little maintenance jobs I probably should be able to manage myself, which makes owning the house more expensive than it needs to be.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “not doing as well,” but it does feel like I’m avoiding things I really ought to be dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would decrease significantly if you were in a completely different environment, like a spider-free zone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help, yes… if I absolutely knew there couldn’t be any spiders, I imagine I’d relax quite a bit.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s only the actual spider, if that makes sense. I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I put off the laundry because it’s in the basement, I avoid the garden shed, and sometimes I even pay someone to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.  \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s more the pattern than travel.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically of spiders, or are you also afraid of other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders, as far as I can tell. I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. And I’ve started paying people to do little maintenance jobs I probably should be able to manage myself, which makes owning the house more expensive than it needs to be.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “not doing as well,” but it does feel like I’m avoiding things I really ought to be dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would decrease significantly if you were in a completely different environment, like a spider-free zone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help, yes… if I absolutely knew there couldn’t be any spiders, I imagine I’d relax quite a bit.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s only the actual spider, if that makes sense. I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. It’s just that as soon as I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nSo yes, a completely spider-free place would likely make it better, but the fear seems to come up very quickly once I think there might be one around. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would still be as intense if you were living in a completely different house or location?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it would be much better somewhere else, and maybe it would be a bit. But I don’t think the house is the whole issue.\n\nI know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer, it just feels… enormous, and threatening, much more than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed admitting that.\n\nSo I suppose in a different house or location it might be less intense, but I’m not sure it would completely go away. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Probably not quite as problematic, maybe, if the house felt newer or easier to check… but I don’t think just being in a different house in the same neighborhood would fix it.

I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would still be as problematic if you were living in the same neighborhood but in a different house?\n\nPatient: Probably not quite as problematic, maybe, if the house felt newer or easier to check… but I don’t think just being in a different house in the same neighborhood would fix it.\n\nI know this sounds a bit silly, but I know a spider in the corner isn’t really going to hurt me. I think it might be a little easier in someone else’s home, especially if it felt tidy or less… you know, old and full of places for them to be. But I don’t think it would disappear completely.

I mean, I do know a spider in the corner probably isn’t going to hurt me. I’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. But if I picture one suddenly dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer or somewhere like that, it just feels enormous and threatening for a moment. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that.

So yes, maybe less intense at a friend’s or family member’s house, but if I thought there might be spiders there, I think the fear would still come up. Is that answering it properly?

I’m not thrilled about insects in general, but they don’t do this to me.\n\nWith spiders, I can know perfectly well that one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I imagine one dropping out of a box or being near the washer… it suddenly feels huge and threatening. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down social invitations because they involved going to a place where you might encounter spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really social invitations, no… at least I don’t think so. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s much more in my own house. I keep sort of arranging things around not seeing spiders — putting off laundry because it’s in the basement, avoiding the garden shed, and sometimes paying someone to do small jobs that I know I’m perfectly capable of doing, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those places.\n\nSo no, not so much turning down people, more… avoiding parts of my own home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of spiders is controlling your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s just a few inconvenient habits, but owning the house has become harder because of it. I let chores build up if they involve places where I think I might see one, and I’ve paid people to do small maintenance things that I really should be able to handle myself.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to sound helpless, but it has started shaping how I manage the house day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: I think… about thirteen months now. It started not long after I moved into the house, when I found those spiders while I was unpacking.\n\nI suppose I hoped it would just settle down once I got used to the place, but it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of spiders occur in places other than your home, such as friends' houses or public areas?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed it much outside my own home.\n\nI mean, if I’m at someone else’s house I suppose the thought can cross my mind, but I can’t honestly say it’s been a big issue in public places or anything like that. It’s mainly tied to my house, from what I can tell. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of spiders still be a problem if you were to move to a different house?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably, though I’d like to think it might be better somewhere newer or cleaner.\n\nIt isn’t that I believe spiders are really out to hurt me. I know, logically, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t dangerous. But if I picture one dropping out of a box or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should.\n\nSo I suppose moving might help a bit, but I’m not sure it would make the fear disappear completely. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or tasks that you feel you're not doing as well as you used to, or that you're having trouble with, because of your fear of spiders?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it’s not as if I can’t do anything.\n\nIt’s more that the house is becoming harder to keep on top of. I’ll put off small chores if they involve certain parts of the house or the property, and then they sort of pile up. And I’ve started paying people to do little maintenance jobs I probably should be able to manage myself, which makes owning the house more expensive than it needs to be.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “not doing as well,” but it does feel like I’m avoiding things I really ought to be dealing with.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would decrease significantly if you were in a completely different environment, like a spider-free zone?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help, yes… if I absolutely knew there couldn’t be any spiders, I imagine I’d relax quite a bit.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s only the actual spider, if that makes sense. I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. It’s just that as soon as I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nSo yes, a completely spider-free place would likely make it better, but the fear seems to come up very quickly once I think there might be one around. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would still be as intense if you were living in a completely different house or location?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it would be much better somewhere else, and maybe it would be a bit. But I don’t think the house is the whole issue.\n\nI know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer, it just feels… enormous, and threatening, much more than it should. I’m a bit embarrassed admitting that.\n\nSo I suppose in a different house or location it might be less intense, but I’m not sure it would completely go away. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would still be as problematic if you were living in the same neighborhood but in a different house?\n\nPatient: Probably not quite as problematic, maybe, if the house felt newer or easier to check… but I don’t think just being in a different house in the same neighborhood would fix it.\n\nI know this sounds a bit silly, but I know a spider in the corner isn’t really going to hurt me. It’s more that if I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer, it suddenly feels much bigger and more threatening than it should. I’m embarrassed saying that, but that’s what seems to happen.\n\nSo yes, the particular house might make a difference, but I think the fear would still come with me to some extent. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of spiders would still be as intense if you were to visit or stay in a friend's or family member's home?\n\nPatient: I think it might be a little easier in someone else’s home, especially if it felt tidy or less… you know, old and full of places for them to be. But I don’t think it would disappear completely.\n\nI mean, I do know a spider in the corner probably isn’t going to hurt me. I’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. But if I picture one suddenly dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer or somewhere like that, it just feels enormous and threatening for a moment. I’m a bit embarrassed saying that.\n\nSo yes, maybe less intense at a friend’s or family member’s house, but if I thought there might be spiders there, I think the fear would still come up. Is that answering it properly?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
